Efficient estimation with incomplete data via generalised ANOVA decompositions
Thomas B. Berrett

Abstract
We study the semiparametric efficient estimation of a class of linear functionals in settings where a complete multivariate dataset is supplemented by additional datasets recording subsets of the variables of interest. These datasets are allowed to have a general, in particular non-monotonic, structure. Our main contribution is to characterise the asymptotic minimal mean squared error for these problems and to introduce an estimator whose risk approximately matches this lower bound. We show that the efficient rescaled variance can be expressed as the minimal value of a quadratic optimisation problem over a function space, thus establishing a fundamental link between these estimation problems and the theory of generalised ANOVA decompositions. Our estimation procedure uses iterated nonparametric regression to mimic an approximate influence function derived through gradient descent. We prove that this estimator is approximately normally distributed, provide an estimator of its variance and thus develop confidence intervals of asymptotically minimal width. Finally we present extensions of our theory demonstrating that the framework can be adapted to include various types of sampling bias and non-linear functionals. Keywords : Semiparametric efficient estimation; Semi-supervised learning; Data fusion; Missing data; Generalised ANOVA.



1
Introduction

Large modern datasets are increasingly often compiled from smaller datasets recording different variables or collected under different experimental conditions. This feature of modern data science is reflected in the vast range of statistical and machine learning literature tackling issues of incompleteness, data heterogeneity and sampling bias, including topics such as semi-supervised learning
(
Chakrabortty and Cai, 2018
;
Kim et al., 2024
)
, data fusion
(
Li and Luedtke, 2023
;
Qiu, Tchetgen Tchetgen and Dobriban, 2024
)
, missing data
(
Robins et al., 2017
)
and transfer learning
(
Cai and Wei, 2021
)
. Areas of application are diverse and cover genomics
(
Angelopoulos et al., 2023
)
, climate science
(
Lucas and Soares, 2015
)
and causal inference
(
Bareinboim and Pearl, 2016
)
. Particularly when clean data is scarce, the information provided by additional data is invaluable.

Relevant literature commonly seeks to derive optimal estimators given various structures of datasets, with statistical efficiency
(
van der Vaart, 2000
, in the sense of, e.g.)
being one of the most widespread notions of optimality. The simplest structures are those in which a complete dataset is supplemented by an additional dataset recording a subset of the variables of interest. These structures arise in semi-supervised settings, where we aim to solve a supervised learning problem with the aid of additional unlabelled data
(
Chakrabortty and Cai, 2018
;
Cannings, Berrett and Samworth, 2020
;
Kim et al., 2024
)
. They also arise in missing data settings, where we aim to carry out a regression analysis when covariates are fully observed and response variables may be missing
(
Robins et al., 2017
)
. In causal inference we may combine a large dataset with unmeasured confounding variables with a smaller dataset recording information on these confounders
(
Yang and Ding, 2020
)
. It is shown in many of these settings that estimators properly incorporating incomplete data have smaller risk than those based solely on the complete data and can sometimes be proved to attain efficiency lower bounds.

Often one would like to combine more than two datasets, though the development of optimal estimators in these settings is typically more complex. In recent examples of work on this problem,
Li and Luedtke (2023)
and
Qiu, Tchetgen Tchetgen and Dobriban (2024)
study efficiency in settings in which a complete dataset is supplemented by multiple incomplete datasets having a nested structure, while allowing for distributional shifts between datasets. A key example giving rise to these structures is that of a longitudinal study, where multiple response variables are recorded over time on the same data subjects who may drop out of the study at various points. In such cases data is available on subsets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
of all variables, with
S_{1}\subseteq S_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq S_{m}
. This monotonic structure enables the derivation of explicit expressions for efficient influence functions and thus the construction of efficient estimators.

However, non-monotonic structures are common in practice and require new techniques. Healthcare datasets such as that collected by the Alzheimer’s Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI)
(
Mueller et al., 2005
)
and MIMIC-III
(
Johnson et al., 2016
)
often exhibit non-monotonic missingness structures in settings where patients may undergo several diagnostic procedures. Certain readings may be refused due to their expense or invasive nature and others may be discarded as inaccurate, leading to complex datasets where different groups of patients provide valuable and complementary information on targets of inference. Data of this type are studied in literature on
block-wise missingness

(
Xue and Qu, 2021
)
, where work on efficient estimation has been developed in parametric models. Multi-phase sampling designs in causal inference can also lead to the combination of multiple datasets with non-monotonic structures
(
Clayton et al., 1998
;
Yang and Ding, 2020
)
. General missing data settings are non-monotonic, though the analysis of such data and the modelling of missingness mechanisms is significantly more challenging when this is the case
(
Robins and Gill, 1997
;
Tsiatis, 2006
;
Sun and Tchetgen Tchetgen, 2018
)
. In such settings it is therefore useful to consider different types of assumptions controlling sampling biases.

1.1
Formal setting and contributions

We will suppose that we have access to a complete
*
*

*

Throughout, when we refer to the ‘complete’ data we mean this fully-observed dataset of size
n
, not the entire dataset of size
n+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}n_{S}
.

d
-dimensional dataset
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}f

for some density function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
. In addition, writing
[d]=\{1,\ldots,d\}
and
2^{[d]}
for the power set of
[d]
, for a collection
\mathbb{S}\subseteq 2^{[d]}\setminus\{[d]\}
we will suppose that we have access to independent incomplete data
(X_{S,i}:S\in\mathbb{S},i\in[n_{S}])
. Here
X_{S,i}
takes values in
(\mathbb{R}\times\{\texttt{NA}\})^{d}
such that the
j
th component of
X_{S,i}
is
NA
if and only if
j\in S^{c}=[d]\setminus S
. We may assume, without loss of generality, that the samples sizes
n_{\mathbb{S}}=(n_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
are positive. We will sometimes use the notation
\mathbb{S}^{+}=\mathbb{S}\cup\{[d]\}
,
n_{[d]}=n
and
X_{[d],i}=X_{i}
so that our data can be summarised as
(X_{S,i}:S\in\mathbb{S}^{+},i\in[n_{S}])
.

The distribution of the incomplete data is assumed to be linked to the target density
f
through a sequence of positive functions
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
representing distributional shifts. Letting
f_{S}
be the density of
(X_{1})_{S}=(X_{1,j}:j\in S)
, that is the marginal density of
f
associated to those variables in
S
, the density of
(X_{S,i})_{S}=(X_{S,i,j}:j\in S)
is proportional to
r_{S}f_{S}
for each
S\in\mathbb{S}
. More formally, we assume that

(X_{S,1})_{S},\ldots,(X_{S,n_{S}})_{S}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\bar{r}_{S}f_{S}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{S,i,j}=\texttt{NA}\text{ almost surely for }j\in S^{c},

(1)

where we write
\bar{r}_{S}=r_{S}/\int fr_{S}
for the normalised version of
r_{S}
. Taking all
r_{S}\equiv 1
we recover the Missing Completely At Random (MCAR) case, but the additional flexibility allows us to specify the
r_{S}
to account for distributional shifts; see the discussion of related work below.

Our aim is to use this data to estimate linear functionals of the form

\theta(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a(x)f(x)\,dx=\mathbb{E}\{a(X)\},

(2)

where
a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a known measurable function and we let
X\sim f
. This canonical statistical problem includes the estimation of moments, probabilities of events and prediction risks. When
\int a^{2}f<\infty
and we only have access to the complete cases in the data, it is known that the natural estimator
\widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{CC}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a(X_{i})
is optimal in the local asymptotic minimax sense. The goal of this work is to use the incomplete data to outperform
\widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{CC}}
, in particular to characterise the minimal asymptotic squared risk for the estimation of
\theta(f)
and develop estimators that attain this optimal risk and thus are statistically efficient. Our main contributions are summarised below.

•

In Section
3
we give a local asymptotic minimax lower bound (Theorem
8
) for the estimation of
\theta(f)
given the data
(X_{S,i}:S\in\mathbb{S}^{+},i\in[n_{S}])
introduced above, in the case that the shift functions
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
are known. This bound can be interpreted as the minimal expected squared error in approximating the function
a(x)
by sums
\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}(x_{S})
of functions of subsets of our variables and thus provides a fundamental link between this estimation problem and the theory of generalised ANOVA decompositions. We introduce and discuss such decompositions in Section
3.1
, state our lower bound in Section
3.2
and give examples in Section
3.3
.

•

In Section
4
we develop an estimator whose rescaled risk, under natural regularity conditions, is close in a nonasymptotic sense to the lower bound in Theorem
8
. In Section
4.1
we take a gradient-descent approach to approximate the generalised ANOVA decomposition of
a(\cdot)
, resulting in an approximate influence function for our estimation problem. In Section
4.2
we introduce a data-driven estimator of
\theta(f)
based on iterated nonparametric regression and bound its risk. In Section
4.3
we prove the approximate normality of our estimator and show how its variance can be estimated to give confidence intervals for
\theta(f)
. Numerical results are given in Section
4.4
.

•

In Section
5
we present extensions of our basic framework to incorporate more complex sampling biases. In Section
5.1
we work under a Missing At Random assumption, assuming that our data is first generated from the target distribution and is then subject to random missingness that may depend on certain covariates. In Section
5.2
we broaden our data model in a different direction, allowing the distributional shift functions
r_{S}
to be unknown elements of a parametric model.

Additionally, in Appendix
A
we study an alternative estimator for the MCAR case that does not require cross-fitting, unlike the estimator in Section
4
. Appendix
B
presents additional numerical results, Appendix
C
discusses extensions to non-linear and two-sample functionals, and proofs and an auxiliary lemma are given in Appendix
D
. Taken together, our results introduce efficient estimators for general structures of incomplete data and allow us to compare the effects of different types of sampling biases on estimation accuracy.

1.2
Related work

Our model is closely related to models for semi-supervised learning, particularly when
\mathbb{S}
is a singleton. There is a vast literature on this topic so here we highlight references that focus on the estimation of mean functionals and statistical efficiency.
The most common setting is that one has access to a dataset of i.i.d. copies of a pair
(X,Y)
, where
Y
is thought of as a label and
X
as a feature vector, as well as an unlabelled dataset recording
X
values only. In this setting,
Zhang, Brown and Cai (2019)
construct estimators of
\mathbb{E}(Y)
that have smaller variance than the natural estimator based only on the labelled data, and also construct estimators that are shown to be efficient under regularity conditions.
Yang and Ding (2020)
and
Cannings and Fan (2022)
consider the estimation of more general functionals, constructing estimators that improve upon the basic estimator and which can be shown to be efficient.
Kim et al. (2024)
use
U
-statistics to estimate functionals of the form
\mathbb{E}\{a(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})\}
, where
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r}
are independent copies of the label
Y
, and provide local asymptotic minimax lower bounds to prove that their estimators are efficient. Beyond mean functionals, we note that there is also work on efficient parameter estimation in semiparametric models in semi-supervised settings
(
Chakrabortty and Cai, 2018
;
Azriel et al., 2022
)
. While the work above focuses on the common setting where
\mathbb{S}
is a singleton,
Yang and Ding (2020)
and
Cannings and Fan (2022)
also consider cases where
|\mathbb{S}|>1
, though their estimators are typically not efficient.

Models similar to semi-supervised settings arise in causal inference and missing data literature, where we may have a regression model with a partially-observed response
Y
and fully-observed covariates
X
and aim to estimate a mean response
\mathbb{E}(Y)
or average treatment effect. Assuming that
\pi(x)=\mathbb{P}(Y\text{ observed }|X=x)^{-1}
is a Hölder smooth function of
x
bounded away from zero and infinity,
Robins et al. (2017)
develop a theory of double robustness and efficiency for the estimation of
\mathbb{E}(Y)
. Work such as
Kallus and Mao (2024)
and
Zhang, Chakrabortty and Bradic (2023)
weakens the assumption that
\pi
is bounded to allow for the regime that the unlabelled dataset is much larger than the labelled dataset.

Statistical efficiency is a key concern in data fusion and missing data models.
Li and Luedtke (2023)
study settings where the joint target distribution
P
is decomposed into conditional distributions
P_{X_{1}}P_{X_{2}|X_{1}}\ldots P_{X_{d}|(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d-1})}
and it is assumed that additional datasets contain accurate information on some of these conditional distributions. In settings such as longitudinal studies with a monotonic missingness structure such that
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\{[1],[2],\ldots,[d-1]\}
, efficient influence functions are explicitly derived. Using similar decompositions of
P
and distributional shift conditions,
Qiu, Tchetgen Tchetgen and Dobriban (2024)
develop multiple-robustness theory for the efficient estimation of functionals of the form
\mathbb{E}\{a(X)\}
. In the causal inference literature it is often of interest to combine the results of a randomised controlled trial with observational data in order to improve the efficiency of estimators; see
Bareinboim and Pearl (2016)
for an introduction to the topic and
Colnet et al. (2024)
;
Lin, Tarp and Evans (2024)
for recent survey articles.

Tackling a non-monotic setting,
Bickel, Ritov and Wellner (1991)
give efficient estimators of mean functionals of bivariate densities whose marginal densities are known, corresponding in our notation to the
d=2
setting with
\mathbb{S}=\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
in the limit that
n_{\{1\}}=n_{\{2\}}=\infty
. Our interest is in finite sample sizes and general structures, though the consideration of
L_{2}
projections is similarly crucial. Their estimators work by binning continuous data, while our methods are based on iterated nonparametric regression with kernels. Shortly after the first version of this work appeared on
arXiv
, the relevant work
Graham, Carone and Rotnitzky (2024)
appeared. This was similarly motivated by the desire to extend existing work, such as
Li and Luedtke (2023)
, to settings with non-monotonic structure. The authors study general data fusion problems where conditional distributions in source populations align with those in the target population and provide tools that can be used to characterise sets of all influence functions. When our settings overlap, their results can be used to verify that the efficient influence functions we derive are indeed the efficient influence functions.
Our work complements this unifying theory by providing, in the settings we consider, more explicit derivations of efficient influence functions and the construction of efficient estimators. While our main interest here is in nonparametric models, we also mention recent work on block-wise missing data
(
Xue and Qu, 2021
;
Song, Lin and Zhou, 2024
;
Li et al., 2024
)
that fits parametric models given data with non-monotonic missingness structures.

Our work also contributes to the literature on distributional shifts and the use of density ratios. In our basic model we assume that the distributional shifts
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
are known, as in previous literature. While primarily concerned with MCAR data, prediction-powered inference extends to settings with known distributional shifts
(
Angelopoulos et al., 2023
)
. In a transfer learning setting with
covariate shift
,
Ma, Pathak and Wainwright (2023)
construct optimal nonparametric regression estimators using knowledge of the density ratio between the target and source covariate distributions.
Tibshirani et al. (2019)
studies conformal prediction under covariate shift, similarly using knowledge of a density ratio.
Thams et al. (2023)
considers a range of hypothesis testing problems where data is drawn from a source distribution being a known shift of the target distribution, including off-policy testing for bandit problems, model selection and the detection of heterogeneity in causal discovery. Here it is common that shifts only affect a subset of the variables, whose distributions are well understood from previous data. Parametric families of shifts, such as those in Section
5.2
, are studied in statistical work such as
Efron and Tibshirani (1996)
;
Qin (1998)
;
Qiu, Tchetgen Tchetgen and Dobriban (2024)
. Density ratios themselves can be estimated directly; see
Sugiyama, Suzuki and Kanamori (2012)
for an introduction to this topic.

Our results show that the efficient influence functions for the problems we consider are found by minimising quadratic objective functions. In limiting cases with incomplete datasets of infinite size this minimisation problem coincides with the problem of finding
generalised ANOVA decompositions

(
Stone, 1994
, e.g.)
. Such decompositions extend classical ANOVA decompositions
(
Hoeffding, 1948
;
Efron and Stein, 1981
)
beyond the setting in which the components of
X
are independent. Work such as
Stone (1994)
and
Huang (1998)
has explored the statistical use of generalised ANOVA decompositions in problems such as nonparametric regression, where the aim is to approximate an unknown regression function by sums of simpler components. Such decompositions are also used in sensitivity analysis
(
Chastaing, Gamboa and Prieur, 2012
;
Rahman, 2014
)
, where the aim is to identify subsets of variables that make a negligible contribution to an outcome variable. Classical ANOVA decompositions have also recently been used as the basis for modelling assumptions in missing data problems
(
Sell, Berrett and Cannings, 2024
)
.
Hooker (2007)
,
Li and Rabitz (2012)
and
Rahman (2014)
further study theoretical properties of generalised ANOVA decompositions and introduce numerical schemes for their computation.

1.3
Notation

Here we collect basic, commonly-used notation. We write
\mathbb{N}
for the set of natural numbers and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
write
[d]=\{1,\ldots,d\}
and write
2^{[d]}
for the collection of all subsets of
[d]
. Given
S\in 2^{[d]}
and
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}
we write
E^{S}=\prod_{j\in S}E
for the marginal space of
E^{d}
corresponding to those variables in
S
. For
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write
\|u\|_{\infty}=\max_{j\in[d]}|u_{j}|
and
\|u\|_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|u_{j}|
, and for a function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we write
\|g\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g(x)|
. For
x\in(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\texttt{NA}\})^{d}
and
S\in 2^{[d]}
we write
x_{S}\equiv(x)_{S}=(x_{j}:j\in S)
for those entries of
x
corresponding to the variables in
S
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in[n]
we write
(n)_{m}=n(n-1)\ldots(n-m+1)
for the falling factorial and
\mathcal{I}_{m}=\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in[n]^{m}:i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\text{ are distinct}\}
, so that
|\mathcal{I}_{m}|=(n)_{m}
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
we write
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
and
a\vee b=\max(a,b)
. For distributions
P
and
Q
with densities
f
and
g
we write
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\int|f-g|
for the Total Variation distance.

2
Problem formulation and main result

Recall from Section
1.1
that for some density
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
we suppose that we have access to a complete dataset
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}f
as well as independent incomplete data
(X_{S,i}:S\in\mathbb{S},i\in[n_{S}])
whose distribution depends on known distributional shifts
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
and is given in (
1
). We aim to estimate the functionals defined in (
2
) for a known function
a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Before stating our main result we gather our assumptions. We will suppose that
\|a\|_{\infty}<\infty
and that there exist constants
0<c\leq C<\infty
such that
c\leq\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})\leq C
for all
x_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
. For fixed parameters
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}>0
we assume the following.

(A1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1}
)

For all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
we have

|a(x)-a(x^{\prime})|\leq L_{1}\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{\beta_{1}}.

(3)

(A2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}
)

For all
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
x_{S},x_{S}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
with
\|x_{S}-x_{S}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
we have

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{L}(X_{S^{c}}|X_{S}=x_{S}),\mathcal{L}(X_{S^{c}}|X_{S}=x_{S}^{\prime}))\leq L_{2}\|x_{S}-x_{S^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}^{\beta_{2}}.

(4)

(A3)(
\beta_{3},L_{3}
)

For all
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
x_{S},x_{S}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
with
\|x_{S}-x_{S}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
we have

|\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})-\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S}^{\prime})|\leq L_{3}\|x_{S}-x_{S}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{\beta_{3}}.

(5)

We assume that
n_{S}/n\rightarrow\lambda_{S}\in(0,\infty)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
for each
S\in\mathbb{S}
and make the technical assumption that
|n\lambda_{S}/n_{S}-1|\leq 1/n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that the conditions of the previous paragraph hold.

(i)

There exists an estimator
\widehat{\theta}\equiv\widehat{\theta}_{n}
such that

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{f}[n\{\widehat{\theta}-\theta(f)\}^{2}]\leq\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}),

where
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
is defined in (
7
).

(ii)

For any measurable estimator sequence
(\theta_{n})
we have

\sup_{I\subset L^{2}}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\max_{h\in I}\mathbb{E}_{f_{n^{-1/2}h}}\bigl[n\bigl\{\theta_{n}-\theta(f_{n^{-1/2}h})\}^{2}\bigr]\geq\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}),

where the supremum is taken over all finite subsets
I
of square-integrable functions
h
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and where the local perturbations
f_{h}
of
f
are defined in Section
3.2
.

The estimator
\widehat{\theta}
in the above statement is introduced in Section
4.2
. The first part of Theorem
1
follows from the finite-sample result Theorem
15
, but we give an asymptotic statement here for brevity. The second part of Theorem
1
is a local asymptotic minimax lower bound following from Theorem
8
. The combination of both parts of Theorem
1
shows that
\widehat{\theta}
is an efficient estimator of
\theta
and that asymptotic minimal risks in this problem are characterised by the penalised generalised ANOVA decompositions introduced in Section
3.1
.

The requirement that
a
be bounded is made to simplify our statement but, as with many of our other assumptions, it can be weakened; this is discussed in the sequel, particularly in the discussion after the statement of Theorem
15
. The assumptions that
\lambda_{S}\in(0,\infty)
and
c\leq r_{S}(x_{S})\leq C
mean that (local) sample sizes across the different datasets are comparable and are commonly made in the literature
(e.g.
Robins et al., 2017
, Assumption 3.1)
. Our assumptions (A1)
(\beta_{1},L_{1})
, (A2)
(\beta_{2},L_{2})
and (A3)
(\beta_{3},L_{3})
concern the smoothness of
a
, of the
\bar{r}_{S}
and of the relevant conditional distributions of
X
, and are used to control the smoothness of shifted conditional expectations of
a
. With this in mind, the first and third assumptions are natural. The second condition is made so that we can control, for example, the difference in conditional expectations
|\mathbb{E}\{a(X)|X_{S}=x_{S}\}-\mathbb{E}\{a(X)|X_{S}=x_{S}^{\prime}\}|
. Such assumptions have been made previously in other contexts; see, for example,
Neykov, Balakrishnan and Wasserman (2021)
.

We conclude this section with examples of problems covered by our setting.

Example 2
.

Consider a study where we wish to estimate the mean response
\mathbb{E}(Y)
given data on
X=(Z,U,Y)
. Full information is available for those subjects in the study, but the covariates in
U
are difficult to collect and unavailable in an additional observational dataset recording
(Z,\texttt{NA},Y)

(
Yang and Ding, 2020
, e.g.)
. We also have access to unlabelled data on all covariates
(Z,U,\texttt{NA})
, as in semi-supervised problems. Here the minimal variance is given by

\inf_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\biggl[\mathrm{Var}\bigl(Y-\alpha_{1}(Z,Y)-\alpha_{2}(Z,U)\bigr)+\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\alpha_{1}(Z,Y)^{2}}{\lambda_{1}\bar{r}_{1}(Z)}\biggr\}+\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\alpha_{2}(Z,U)^{2}}{\lambda_{2}\bar{r}_{2}(Z,U)}\biggr\}\biggr]

where
\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2}
allow for covariate shift between the main study and the observational data.

Example 3
.

Two diagnostic tests are available to measure the severity of a health condition
(
Xue and Qu, 2021
, e.g.)
and a predictive algorithm
m(Z_{1},Z_{2})
has been built for a response
Y
from the two measurements. We want to estimate the squared prediction error based on data from individuals who underwent one or both of the tests, so that we have data on
(Z_{1},Z_{2},Y),(Z_{1},\texttt{NA},Y)
and
(\texttt{NA},Z_{2},Y)
. The minimal variance here is given by

\inf_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\biggl[\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\{Y-m(Z_{1},Z_{2})\}^{2}-\alpha_{1}(Z_{1},Y)-\alpha_{2}(Z_{2},Y)\bigr)+\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\alpha_{1}(Z_{1},Y)^{2}}{\lambda_{1}\bar{r}_{1}(Z_{1},Y)}\biggr\}+\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\alpha_{2}(Z_{2},Y)^{2}}{\lambda_{2}\bar{r}_{2}(Z_{2},Y)}\biggr\}\biggr],

where
\bar{r}_{1},\bar{r}_{2}
allow for changes in distributions between patients having both tests and those refusing or being advised against one.

In missing data literature it is sometimes preferred to work with a fixed total sample size and data that is randomly missing. Our results adapt to such settings. We give a corollary of Theorem
1
in the MCAR case below and extensions to MAR cases in Section
5.1
. Suppose that there is a collection of i.i.d., potentially unobserved data
(Y_{1},\Omega_{1}),\ldots,(Y_{N},\Omega_{N})
where
Y_{1}\sim f
and
\Omega_{1}
takes values in
\{0,1\}^{d}
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{j}=1\,\forall j)>0
. We observe
Y_{i}\circ\Omega_{i}
where

(y\circ\omega)_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}y_{j}&\text{if }\omega_{j}=1\\
\texttt{NA}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

For
S\in 2^{[d]}
let
\mathbbm{1}_{S}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
be the indicator vector such that
(\mathbbm{1}_{S})_{j}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{j\in S\}}
, write
p_{S}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega=\mathbbm{1}_{S})
and let
\mathbb{S}=\{S\subset[d]:p_{S}>0\}
. Assuming that data is MCAR, in the sense that
Y\perp\!\!\!\perp\Omega
, then the conditional density of
Y_{S}|\{\Omega=\mathbbm{1}_{S}\}
is given by
f_{S}
. Writing
N_{S}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\Omega_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{S}\}}
, on the event that the
N_{\mathbb{S}}=(N_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
are not too small, we can apply the estimator constructed in Section
4
with
r_{S}\equiv 1
and
n_{S}=N_{S}
for each
S\in\mathbb{S}
. The following result justifies the efficiency of this procedure in this missing data model.

Corollary 4
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
1
hold. Let
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
be defined as in (
7
) with
\lambda_{S}=p_{S}/p_{[d]}
and
r_{S}\equiv 1
for each
S\in\mathbb{S}
. Then there exists an estimator
\widehat{\Theta}
such that

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{f}[N\{\widehat{\Theta}-\theta(f)\}^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{p_{[d]}}\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}).

(6)

A corresponding lower bound is proved using similar arguments to those used to prove Theorem
8
. Alternatively, we can specialise Theorem
17
below, where we consider MAR data. In the example below we apply Corollary
4
to a common model for missing data.

Example 5
.

Using the notation introduced above, suppose that each variable is missing independently, so that for some
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\in(0,1]
and for any
S\subseteq[d]
we may write
p_{S}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega=\mathbbm{1}_{S})=\{\prod_{j\in S}p_{j}\}\{\prod_{j\notin S}(1-p_{j})\}
. Then the minimal asymptotic risk on the right-hand side of (
6
) specialises to

\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{p_{[d]}}\mathrm{Var}\biggl(a(X)-\sum_{S\subset[d]}\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\biggr)+\sum_{S\subset[d]}\frac{1}{p_{S}}\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\bigr)\biggr\}.

3
Lower bound

3.1
Generalised ANOVA decompositions

Generalised ANOVA decompositions
(
Stone, 1994
, e.g.)
are used to model and approximate complex functions
a(X)
of dependent variables, extending work
(
Hoeffding, 1948
;
Efron and Stein, 1981
)
on decompositions for functions of independent variables.
In order to formally discuss such decompositions we require some notation. For
S\subset[d]
write

L^{2}=\biggl\{\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\int\alpha^{2}f<\infty\biggr\}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{S}=\biggl\{\alpha_{S}\in L^{2}:\alpha_{S}(x)\equiv\alpha_{S}(x_{S}),\int\alpha_{S}f=0\biggr\}

for the set of all square-integrable functions with respect to the probability density function
f
and the linear subspace thereof consisting of all mean-zero functions that do not depend on those
x_{j}
for which
j\not\in S
. When equipped with the inner product
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int\alpha\beta f
it is well known that
L^{2}
is a Hilbert space. Write
H_{\mathbb{S}}=\prod_{S\in\mathbb{S}}H_{S}
for the product of these subspaces. Given a function
a\in L^{2}
, we may then aim to minimise
\mathrm{Var}(a(X)-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}(X_{S}))
over
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}=(\alpha_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})\in H_{\mathbb{S}}
.
Under natural orthogonality conditions on
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}
and regularity conditions on
f
, it can be shown
(e.g.
Stone, 1994
, Theorem 3.1)
that there is a unique minimiser
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}
, and we call
\int af+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}^{*}
the generalised ANOVA decomposition of
a
.

In our setting, a modification of this optimisation problem allows us to characterise optimal estimators of
\theta(f)=\int af
. Recalling that
\lambda_{S}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n_{S}/n
, define the objective function

\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})=\Var\biggl(a(X)-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\biggr)+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\alpha_{S}(X_{S})^{2}}{\lambda_{S}\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S})}\biggr],

(7)

which can be considered as a naturally penalised version of the objective function in the previous paragraph. Our first result shows that
\mathcal{L}
has a unique minimiser whenever these penalties are non-zero and the
\bar{r}_{S}
are bounded. The existence and uniqueness of a minimiser is crucial to our proof of the local asymptotic minimax lower bound in Theorem
8
, and is convenient in the upper bound arguments in Section
4
.

Proposition 6
.

There exists a unique
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*})=\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
.

We conclude this section by giving an explicit minimiser in the case that our variables are independent. While this would be rare in practice, it provides intuition and a link to classical ANOVA decompositions. We can express the optimal
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}
in terms of suitably-centred conditional expectations of
a
that are mutually orthogonal. To this end, for
T\subseteq[d]
write
a_{T}(x_{T})=\mathbb{E}\{a(X)|X_{T}=x_{T}\}
. There do not appear to be simple generalisations to nonconstant
r_{\mathbb{S}}
, even when they have product form, due to the fact that the penalty terms in the objective function do not have orthogonal decompositions that align with the orthogonal decomposition of the main term.

Proposition 7
.

Suppose that
X
has a product distribution and that
r_{S}\equiv 1
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. For
T\subseteq[d]
inductively define
\tilde{a}_{\emptyset}=\theta
and
\tilde{a}_{T}=a_{T}-\sum_{U\subset T}\tilde{a}_{U}
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
is minimised by taking

\alpha_{S}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq T\subseteq S}\frac{\lambda_{S}}{1+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}:T\subseteq S^{\prime}}\lambda_{S^{\prime}}}\tilde{a}_{T}

(8)

for each
S\in\mathbb{S}
.

3.2
Local asymptotic minimax lower bound

Our next result establishes a link between the minimisation of
\mathcal{L}
and our estimation problem. Write
k(u)=1/2+(1+e^{-4u})^{-1}
and for
h\in L^{2}
let
f_{h}
be the perturbation of
f
such that
f_{h}(x)\propto k(h(x))f(x)
and
f_{h}
is a density function. This is a convenient choice of
k
since it is smooth, bounded away from zero and infinity and satisfies
k(0)=k^{\prime}(0)=1
and
k^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
.

Theorem 8
.

Writing
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}
for the minimiser of (
7
) and
\alpha^{*}=a-\theta-\sum_{S}\alpha_{S}^{*}
, for any estimator sequence
(\theta_{n})
we have

\sup_{I\subset\mathbb{R}}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\max_{t\in I}\,\,\mathbb{E}_{f_{n^{-1/2}t\alpha^{*}}}\bigl[n\bigl\{\theta_{n}-\theta(f_{n^{-1/2}t\alpha^{*}})\}^{2}\bigr]\geq\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}),

where the supremum is taken over all finite subsets
I
of
\mathbb{R}
.

When combined with our later upper bounds this result shows that, under suitable regularity conditions, the minimal asymptotic variance is given by
n^{-1}\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
. As well as establishing our lower bound, the result shows that
\alpha^{*}
is the direction in which we should perturb
f
for a least-favourable submodel and thus informs the construction of optimal estimators to follow in Section
4
. In the language of efficiency theory, this means that
\alpha^{*}
is the efficient influence function for the parameter
\theta(P)
in our model.

In order to provide some intuition for this efficient influence function we give formal calculations for perturbations in a general direction, restricting attention to bounded perturbations for simplicity. For a bounded function
h
satisfying
\int hf=0
write
P_{n,h}
for the distribution of the data when
f
is replaced by
f(1+n^{-1/2}h)
and when
n
is sufficiently large that this is non-negative. Writing
h_{S}(x)\equiv h_{S}(x_{S})=\mathbb{E}\{h(X)|X_{S}=x_{S}\}
, the density of
(X_{S,1})_{S}
under
P_{n,h}
is proportional to
r_{S}f_{S}(1+n^{-1/2}h_{S})
and
we therefore see that

\displaystyle\log\frac{dP_{n,h}}{dP_{n,0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bigl(1+n^{-1/2}h(X_{i})\bigr)+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\log\biggl(\frac{1+n^{-1/2}h_{S}(X_{S,j})}{1+n^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\{h_{S}(X_{S,1})\}}\biggr)

\displaystyle=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i})-\frac{1}{2}\Var\{h(X_{1})\}+n^{-1/2}\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\bigl[h_{S}(X_{S,j})-\mathbb{E}\{h_{S}(X_{S,j})\}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\lambda_{S}\Var\{h_{S}(X_{S,j})\}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, under
P_{n,0}
. Thus, writing
\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}=(\lambda_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
, if we define the inner product

\langle h,h^{\prime}\rangle_{\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}}=\mathbb{E}\{h(X)h^{\prime}(X)\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\lambda_{S}\Cov\bigl(h_{S}(X_{S,1}),h_{S}^{\prime}(X_{S,1})\bigr),

(9)

we have that
\log\frac{dP_{n,h}}{dP_{n,0}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\rightarrow}\|h\|_{\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}}Z-(1/2)\|h\|_{\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}}^{2}
for a standard Gaussian variable
Z
, so that our sequence of experiments in locally asymptotically normal. On the other hand, we have
\theta(f(1+n^{-1/2}h))-\theta(f)=n^{-1/2}\int ahf
. According to local asymptotic minimax theory, we should therefore choose
h
to maximise
\int ahf
under the constraint
\|h\|_{\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}}\leq 1
. This is done by finding the adjoint of the map
\dot{\kappa}:h^{\prime}\mapsto\int afh^{\prime}
, i.e. by choosing
h
proportional to the function
\dot{\kappa}^{*}
that satisfies
\langle\dot{\kappa}^{*},h^{\prime}\rangle_{\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}}=\dot{\kappa}(h^{\prime})
for all
h^{\prime}
. We show in the proof of Theorem
8
that the stationarity conditions for minimising
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
ensure that
\alpha^{*}
indeed satisfies this property.

3.3
Examples and special cases

We conclude this section with illustrative examples and special cases to aid the interpretation of the limiting variance
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
. For the simplest structures, where
\mathbb{S}=\{S\}
is a singleton, elementary calculations show that the optimisation problem can be solved by taking

\alpha_{S}(x_{S})=\frac{\lambda_{S}\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})}{1+\lambda_{S}\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{a(X)-\theta^{\prime}|X_{S}=x_{S}\bigr\},

(10)

for
\theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{E}\{\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\}=0
. Our next result extends these calculations to general monotonic structures of
\mathbb{S}
.

Proposition 9
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\{[1],[2],\ldots,[d-1]\}
. Using the shorthand
Y_{j}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{j})
and
y_{j}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j})
we inductively define the functions
R_{j}(y_{j})=\lambda_{[j]}\bar{r}_{[j]}(y_{j})
,
\mu_{d-1}(y_{d-1})\equiv 1
,
\tilde{a}_{d-1}(y_{d-1})=a_{[d-1]}(y_{d-1})-\theta
,
\nu_{j}^{j-1}(y_{j})\equiv 1
,

\displaystyle\mu_{j}(y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\mu_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})}{1+R_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})\mu_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})}\biggm|Y_{j}=y_{j}\biggr\},

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{j}(y_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\mu_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})\tilde{a}_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})}{1+R_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})\mu_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})}\biggm|Y_{j}=y_{j}\biggr\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\nu_{j}^{k}(y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\frac{\nu_{j+1}^{k}(Y_{j+1})}{1+R_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})\mu_{j+1}(Y_{j+1})}\biggm|Y_{j}=y_{j}\biggr\}

for
j\in[d-1]
and
k\geq j
. For notational convenience we write
\nu_{j}^{k}=0
for
k<j-1
, write
\nu_{0}^{k}\equiv 0
for all
k
and write
\tilde{a}_{0}\equiv 0
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
is minimised by taking

\alpha_{[j]}=\frac{R_{j}}{1/\mu_{j}+R_{j}}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\biggl(\prod_{m=k}^{j-1}\frac{1}{1+R_{m}\mu_{m}}\biggr)\biggl\{\tilde{a}_{k}-\tilde{a}_{k-1}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\theta_{\ell}\biggl(\frac{\nu_{k}^{\ell-1}}{\mu_{k}}-\frac{\nu_{k-1}^{\ell-1}}{\mu_{k-1}}\biggr)\biggr\},

(11)

for each
j\in[d-1]
such that
[j]\in\mathbb{S}
, where
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d-1}\in\mathbb{R}
are constants such that
\theta_{j}=0
if
[j]\not\in\mathbb{S}
and such that each
\alpha_{[j]}
has mean zero.

While the optimal
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}
given in Proposition
9
is rather complex, it can be written as a sum of finitely many terms, each of which can be expressed through a finite number of conditional expectations. This is due to the monotonic structure of
\mathbb{S}
, which simplifies the optimisation problem given by (
7
) and allows for a natural sequential approach. To gain some intuition for this minimiser and the quantities involved in its definition we specialise Proposition
9
to the MCAR setting in the following corollary. We also give a simple direct proof.

Corollary 10
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\{[1],[2],\ldots,[d-1]\}
and that
r_{S}\equiv 1
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
is minimised by taking

\alpha_{[j]}=\sum_{k=1}^{j}\frac{\lambda_{[j]}}{1+\sum_{\ell=k}^{d-1}\lambda_{[\ell]}}(a_{[k]}-a_{[k-1]})

for each
j
such that
[j]\in\mathbb{S}
.

When we move beyond monotonic structures, it is not possible to give explicit minimisers in such generality. However, the following example exhibits a non-monotonic setting in which our results give simple expressions for optimal variances and influence functions.

Example 11
.

Write
X=(Y,Z)
where
Y
is a binary response taking values in
\{0,1\}
and
Z
is a covariate taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, with
p=d-1
, and write
\eta(z)=\mathbb{P}(Y=1|Z=z)
. Suppose that
\mathbb{S}=\{\{1\},[d]\setminus\{1\}\}
so that we have incomplete data on
Y
only and incomplete data on
Z
only and write
r_{Y},r_{Z}
for the associated distributional shifts. Consider the problem of estimating
\theta=\mathbb{E}(Y)
. The restriction to binary responses means that the only mean-zero functions
\beta
of
Y
are of the form
\beta(Y)=\mu(Y-\theta)
. We therefore choose
\alpha
and
\mu
to minimise

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\{Y-\theta-\alpha(Z)-\mu(Y-\theta)\}^{2}+\frac{\alpha(Z)^{2}}{\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda_{Y}\bar{r}_{Y}(Y)}(Y-\theta)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{(1-\mu)^{2}+\!\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda_{Y}\bar{r}_{Y}(Y)}\biggr\}(Y-\theta)^{2}+\biggl\{1\!+\!\frac{1}{\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}\biggr\}\alpha(Z)^{2}-2(1-\mu)\{\eta(Z)-\theta\}\alpha(Z)\biggr].

For each fixed
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, as for (
10
) it can be seen that this is minimised by taking
\alpha=(1-\mu)\frac{\lambda\bar{r}_{Z}}{1+\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}}(\eta-\bar{\theta})
,
where
\bar{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen such that
\alpha
has mean zero. It therefore follows that in this example

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{(1-\mu)^{2}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda_{Y}\bar{r}_{Y}(Y)}\biggr\}(Y-\theta)^{2}-(1-\mu)^{2}\frac{\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}{1+\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}\{\eta(Z)-\bar{\theta}\}^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\biggl[\frac{1}{\theta(1-\theta)-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\frac{\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}{1+\lambda_{Z}\bar{r}_{Z}(Z)}\{\eta(Z)-\bar{\theta}\}^{2}\bigr]}+\frac{\lambda_{Y}}{\mathbb{E}\{\frac{(Y-\theta)^{2}}{\bar{r}_{Y}(Y)}\}}\biggr]^{-1}.

When
r_{Z},r_{Y}\equiv 1
, this final expression simplifies to the harmonic mean of
\mathbb{E}\{\mathrm{Var}(Y|Z)\}+(1+\lambda_{Z})^{-1}\mathrm{Var}(\eta(Z))
and
\lambda_{Y}^{-1}\mathrm{Var}(Y)
.

4
Upper bound

In this section we construct estimators whose variance is approximately equal to the minimal asymptotic variance
n^{-1}\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
. We do this by finding an approximate influence function for our problem (in Section
4.1
), which will depend on unknown properties of
f
, and then approximating this in a data-driven fashion using cross fitting (in Section
4.2
). Finally, in Section
4.3
, we will use this development to introduce confidence intervals for
\theta
. Our lower bounds in the previous section were naturally asymptotic, but the results in this section are valid with finite sample sizes. We present a simulation study in Section
4.4
.

4.1
Construction of an approximate influence function

As shown by Theorem
8
, the efficient influence function for our problem is given by
\alpha^{*}=a-\theta-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}^{*}
, where
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}
is the minimiser of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
. This has no general explicit form, but we will see that it can be approximated by repeated use of a simple technique. Choosing a step size
\eta\in(0,1]
and initialising at
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(0)}=0
, we iteratively define
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(m)}
by

\alpha_{S}^{(m+1)}(x_{S})=(1-\eta)\alpha_{S}^{(m)}(x_{S})+\eta\frac{\lambda_{S}\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})}{1+\lambda_{S}\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})}\mathbb{E}\biggl\{a(X)-\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}\setminus\{S\}}\alpha_{S^{\prime}}^{(m)}(X_{S^{\prime}})-\theta^{\prime}\biggm|X_{S}=x_{S}\biggr\},

where
\theta^{\prime}
is chosen to centre the random variable
\alpha_{S}^{(m+1)}(X_{S})
. In view of (
10
), when
\eta=1
this can be seen as applying the naive method, which treats all incomplete datasets separately, to the residuals of the current approximation to
a(\cdot)
. Our next result is proved by showing that this iterative scheme can be thought of as gradient descent in a suitable linear space. This allows us to use techniques from convex optimisation to bound
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)})-\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*})
.

Proposition 12
.

Write
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}=\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\lambda_{S}
and recall that we assume that
\|\bar{r}_{S}\|_{\infty}\leq C
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. Choosing
\eta=|\mathbb{S}|^{-1}
and writing
\kappa=|\mathbb{S}|(1+C\lambda_{\mathrm{max}})
we have

\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)})-\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*})\leq\kappa(1-1/\kappa)^{M}\mathrm{Var}\,a(X)

for all
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M+1)})\leq\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)})
for all
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

In the proof of this result we use the smoothness and strong convexity of our problem to show that the value of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
decreases exponentially quickly with a rate governed by the condition number
\kappa
. The strong convexity of the problem follows from the inclusion of the penalty terms in the objective function, which leads to the dependence of
\kappa
on
C\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}
.

4.2
Data-driven estimator

We now turn to the construction of data-driven approximations of
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)}
. It will be convenient to define the approximate oracle estimator

\theta^{*,(M)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{a(X_{i})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}^{(M)}(X_{i})\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{1}{n_{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\frac{\alpha_{S}^{(M)}(X_{S,j})}{\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S,j})}

(12)

that we will aim to mimic with our data. By construction, each
\alpha_{S}^{(M)}
has mean zero under
f
and, since the density of
(X_{S,1})_{S}
is given by
\bar{r}_{S}f_{S}
, we see that
\theta^{*,(M)}
is unbiased for
\theta
. We can show that the variance of
\theta^{*,(M)}
is approximately minimal using Proposition
12
and arguing that
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
is insensitive to small changes in
\lambda_{\mathbb{S}}
, as we do in the proof of Proposition
13
below. The latter is a technical but simple point, arising only because we have
n_{S}/n\rightarrow\lambda_{S}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
rather than equality.

We will approximate
\theta^{*,(M)}
using cross fitting. Write
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\}
for the first half of the complete data and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\{X_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
for the second half. Let
\widehat{r}_{S,(\ell)}=|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}|^{-1}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}r_{S}(x)
and let
\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}(\cdot)
be a function that is calculated using the
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
data only, for
\ell=1,2
, to be constructed below. Define

\widehat{\theta}_{(\ell)}^{(M)}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}|}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}}\biggl\{a(x)-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}(x)\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{1}{n_{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\frac{\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}(X_{S,j})\widehat{r}_{S,(3-\ell)}}{r_{S}(X_{S,j})}

and
\widehat{\theta}=(|\mathcal{D}_{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{(1)}^{(M)}+|\mathcal{D}_{1}|\widehat{\theta}_{(2)}^{(M)})/n
. The next result shows that
\widehat{\theta}
approximates
\theta^{*,(M)}
well whenever the
\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}
are good estimators of the
\alpha_{S}^{(M)}
and establishes basic properties of
\theta^{*,(M)}
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
C>0
be such that
\|\bar{r}_{S}\|_{\infty}\leq C
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. We have that

\mathbb{E}\bigl\{(\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*,(M)})^{2}\bigr\}\leq\frac{8C|\mathbb{S}|}{n}\biggl[\max_{\ell=1,2}\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\int\frac{n+n_{S}\bar{r}_{S}}{n_{S}\bar{r}_{S}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}-\alpha_{S}^{(M)})^{2}f_{S}\biggr\}+\mathrm{Var}(\theta^{*,(M)})\biggr].

Moreover,

\mathrm{Var}(\theta^{*,(M)})\leq\frac{1}{n}\biggl(1+\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\biggl|\frac{n\lambda_{S}}{n_{S}}-1\biggr|\biggr)\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)})

and
\theta^{*,(M)}
is an unbiased estimator of
\theta
.

It now remains to construct the
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S},(\ell)}^{(M)}
as estimators of
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)}
. We follow the iterative definition of
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(M)}
, replacing conditional expectations with nonparametric regression and employing further sample splitting to ease the analysis. Write
\mathcal{D}_{\ell,m}
for the
m
th piece of the
\ell
th half, for
m=1,2,\ldots,M
and
\ell=1,2
. To lessen the notational complexity we focus on the
\ell=1
half of the data and suppress the dependence on
\ell
in the following notation. Write
K(z)=2^{-d}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\|z\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}
for the uniform kernel. Given
S\in\mathbb{S}
and a bandwidth
h\in(0,1)
write
K^{S}(u)\equiv K^{S}(u_{S})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S^{c}}}K(u_{S},u_{S^{c}}^{\prime})\,du_{S^{c}}^{\prime}
and
K_{h}^{S}(u)\equiv K_{h}^{S}(u_{S})=h^{-|S|}K^{S}(u_{S}/h)
. Take
\widehat{r}_{S}^{(m)}=|\mathcal{D}_{1,m}|^{-1}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{1,m}}r_{S}(x)
and
\widehat{f}_{S}^{(m)}(\cdot)=|\mathcal{D}_{1,m}|^{-1}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{1,m}}K_{h}^{S}(\cdot-x)
. Initialising at
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S},(1)}^{(0)}\equiv 0
, we then define
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S},(1)}^{(m)}
for
m=1,\ldots,M
via

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(1)}^{(m)}(x_{S})=\eta\frac{\lambda_{S}r_{S}(x_{S})}{\widehat{r}_{S}^{(m)}+\lambda_{S}r_{S}(x_{S})}\biggl[|\mathcal{D}_{1,m}|^{-1}\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{1,m}}\biggl\{a(y)-\sum_{S^{\prime}\neq S}\widehat{\alpha}_{S^{\prime},(1)}^{(m-1)}(y)\biggr\}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{T}}(y)\frac{K_{h}^{S}(x_{S}-y_{S})}{\widehat{f}_{S}^{(m)}(x_{S})}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\frac{\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{1,m}}\frac{\lambda_{S}r_{S}(y)}{\widehat{r}_{S}^{(m)}+\lambda_{S}r_{S}(y)}\{a(y)-\sum_{S^{\prime}\neq S}\widehat{\alpha}_{S^{\prime},(1)}^{(m-1)}(y)\}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{T}}(y)}{\sum_{y\in\mathcal{D}_{1,m}}\frac{\lambda_{S}r_{S}(y)}{\widehat{r}_{S}^{(m)}+\lambda_{S}r_{S}(y)}}\biggr]+(1-\eta)\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(1)}^{(m-1)}(x_{S})

when
\|x_{S}\|_{\infty}\leq T
and take
\widehat{\alpha}_{(\mathbf{S},S)}^{(m+1)}(x_{S})=0
otherwise. Here we use the convention that
0/0=0
to deal with the event that
\widehat{f}_{S}^{(m+1)}(x_{S})=0
and take
B_{T}=\{x:\|x\|_{\infty}\leq T\}
for some
T\geq 1
to be chosen later. Note that, when
r_{S}\equiv 1
, the centring term in the iteration can be omitted, as the final estimator
\widehat{\theta}
is then invariant to constant additions to the
\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(1)}^{(M)}
.
Our next result controls the error of the estimators
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S},(1)}^{(M)}
.

Proposition 14
.

Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in(0,1]
and
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\in(0,\infty)
, and suppose that
a
, the distribution of
X
and
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
satisfy (A1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1}
), (A2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}
) and (A3)(
\beta_{3},L_{3}
), respectively, and write
\beta_{\wedge}=\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2}\wedge\beta_{3}
. Suppose further that
c\leq\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})\leq C
for all
x_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
. Then for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
we have

\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\int(\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(1)}^{(M)}-\alpha_{S}^{(M)})^{2}f_{S}\biggr\}\leq A^{M}B\biggl\{\frac{MT^{d}}{nh^{d}}+h^{2\beta_{\wedge}}+\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq T)\biggr\},

where
A=28(C/c+2^{d})
and
B=16\max(\|a\|_{\infty}^{2},1)\max\{(C/c)^{2},(L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3})^{2},8^{d}\}
.

The proof of this result is based on a careful analysis of iterated nonparametric regression. This relies on the assumptions (A1)
(\beta_{1},L_{1})
, (A2)
(\beta_{2},L_{2})
and (A3)
(\beta_{3},L_{3})
made in (
3
), (
4
) and (
5
), respectively. Lemma
24
and (
42
) in the proof of Proposition
14
in Section
D.2
show that these assumptions imply the smoothness of the
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{(m)}
.

The combination of Propositions
12
,
13
and
14
yields our main result on the risk of
\widehat{\theta}
.

Theorem 15
.

Suppose that the conditions of Propositions
12
and
14
hold and suppose further that
|n\lambda_{S}/n_{S}-1|\leq 1/n
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. When
h\in[T(M/n)^{1/d},1]
we have

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}\{(\widehat{\theta}-\theta)^{2}\}-\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*})\leq A^{M}D\biggl\{\frac{MT^{d}}{nh^{d}}+h^{2\beta_{\wedge}}+\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq T)\biggr\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 250.0pt+4\kappa\{\mathrm{Var}\,a(X)\}\exp(-M/\kappa).

where
D=200|\mathbb{S}|^{2}(C/c)(1+\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})B
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}=\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\lambda_{S}
. In particular, if all problem parameters are held fixed then

\mathbb{E}\{(\widehat{\theta}-\theta)^{2}\}=\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{*})+o(1/n)

whenever
h,T,M
satisfy
A^{2M}\{MT^{d}/(nh^{d})+h^{2\beta_{\wedge}}+\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq T)\}\rightarrow 0
and
T,M\rightarrow\infty
.

If there exists
\rho>0
such that
\mathbb{E}(\|X\|_{\infty}^{\rho})<\infty
then we may choose, for example,
h=1/T=n^{-1/(4d)}
and
M=\sqrt{\log n}
to satisfy the final condition in Theorem
15
. However, this moment condition can be weakened by choosing
M
to diverge more slowly. Indeed, for any fixed distribution of
X
we have
\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq n^{1/(4d)})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and we can choose
M
to diverge sufficiently slowly that
A^{2M}\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq n^{1/(4d)})\rightarrow 0
. It would also be possible to weaken our smoothness assumptions to require less than Hölder smoothness, though this would require changes to the proof of Proposition
14
.

The proof of Proposition
14
reveals that our assumptions on
a,f
and
(r_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
only need to hold for
x\in B_{T}
. We could weaken them to allow
C,c,L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},\|a\|_{\infty}
to diverge with
T
, provided we can control the error bound in Theorem
15
. For example, if we have that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{a(x)}{\|x\|_{\infty}\vee 1}\leq 1
and all other constants remain bounded, we can make choices of
h,T,M
such that the error converges to zero if we have
\mathbb{E}(\|X\|_{\infty}^{\rho})<\infty
for some
\rho>4
.

4.3
Confidence intervals

In this section we show how the development in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
can be used to give confidence intervals for
\theta
which, given the results of Section
3
, will be of asymptotically minimal width. It follows from Proposition
13
and the fact that
\theta^{*,(M)}
, defined in (
12
), is an average of independent random variables with finite variance that our estimator
\widehat{\theta}
is approximately normally distributed. To construct confidence intervals we therefore need only an estimator of
n\mathrm{Var}(\theta^{*,(M)})
which, by Proposition
13
, approaches the optimal variance
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
for large
M
and
n
. This can be achieved by using similar techniques to those used in Section
4.2
. Indeed, for
\ell=1,2
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
define

\widehat{V}^{(\ell)}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}|}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}}\biggl\{a(x)-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}(x)\biggr\}^{2}-(\widehat{\theta})^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{V}_{S}^{(\ell)}=\frac{n}{|\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}|}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{D}_{3-\ell}}\frac{\widehat{r}_{S,(\ell)}\widehat{\alpha}_{S,(\ell)}^{(M)}(x)^{2}}{n_{S}r_{S}(x)},

where we suppress the dependence of these estimators on
M
and
h
for notational convenience. Our final estimator of the variance is then

\widehat{V}=\frac{1}{2}\biggl\{\widehat{V}^{(1)}+\widehat{V}^{(2)}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}(\widehat{V}_{S}^{(1)}+\widehat{V}_{S}^{(2)})\biggr\}\vee 0

and, writing
z_{\alpha/2}
for the upper
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution, we define the interval
C_{\alpha}=[\widehat{\theta}-n^{-1/2}\widehat{V}^{1/2}z_{\alpha/2},\widehat{\theta}+n^{-1/2}\widehat{V}^{1/2}z_{\alpha/2}]
. We now prove that this is an asymptotically-valid
100(1-\alpha)\%
confidence interval for
\theta
. Writing
d_{\mathrm{K}}
for the Kolmogorov distance we have the following result.

Proposition 16
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
15
hold and that
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})\geq V_{0}>0
. Then we have that

d_{\mathrm{K}}\biggl(\frac{n^{1/2}(\widehat{\theta}-\theta)}{\widehat{V}^{1/2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\widehat{V}>0\}},Z\biggr)\leq\frac{7A^{M/2}D}{V_{0}}\biggl\{\frac{MT^{d}}{nh^{d}}+h^{2\beta_{\wedge}}+\mathbb{P}(\|X\|_{\infty}\geq T)\biggr\}^{1/4}.

In particular,
\mathbb{P}(C_{\alpha}\ni\theta)\rightarrow 1-\alpha
uniformly over
\alpha\in(0,1)
whenever this right-hand side converges to zero.

We see that the validity of these confidence intervals does not require large
M
. However, the proof of this result reveals that
\widehat{V}
is approximately equal to
n\mathrm{Var}(\theta^{*,(M)})
, which only converges to its minimal value as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Since the width of
C_{\alpha}
is given by
2(\widehat{V}/n)^{1/2}z_{\alpha/2}
, a larger value of
M
results in asymptotically shorter confidence intervals. This result relies on the assumption that
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})\geq V_{0}>0
, which is natural given that we must estimate and divide by
\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})
to standardise our estimator. The proof follows from Theorem
15
and a Berry–Esseen bound together with an analysis of the estimator
\widehat{V}
.

4.4
Numerical examples

We conclude this section with a brief simulation study
†
†

†

Code to reproduce the experiments is available on the author’s website
thomasberrett.github.io
.
to demonstrate the feasibility and potential gains in accuracy of the proposed methodology. In each of three settings we suppose that our complete data are i.i.d. copies of some
(X_{1},X_{2},Y)
taking values in
[0,1]^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
, that we have incomplete data of the form
(X_{1},\texttt{NA},\texttt{NA})
and
(\texttt{NA},X_{2},\texttt{NA})
under MCAR, and that we wish to estimate the expected response
\theta=\mathbb{E}(Y)
. We will compare the performance of the complete-case estimator
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
to two estimators constructed to mimic the
M=1,2,3,4
approximate oracle estimators (
12
), where one is the cross-fitting estimator constructed above and the second is a more direct approach similar to that introduced in Section
A
in the appendix. The estimators using
M=1
are of a relatively standard type studied in semi-supervised settings
(
Yang and Ding, 2020
;
Cannings and Fan, 2022
, e.g.)
, where the complete-case estimator is corrected by its conditional expectations, while the
M=2,3,4
estimators exploit dependence between
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
to further reduce the asymptotic risk. For both approaches a bandwidth parameter
h=0.1
is used with a Gaussian kernel in the
R
function
ksmooth
, and we choose step size
\eta=1
. As an additional baseline method we also compare to taking the empirical average of
Y
over a multiply-imputed dataset, where Multivariate Imputation by Chained Equations (MICE)
(
Van Buuren and Groothuis-Oudshoorn, 2011
)
with default options is used for the imputation.

The data are generated as follows:

(i)

For
\rho\in\{0,0.1,0.2,\ldots,1\}
let
(Z_{1},Z_{2})
have standard normal marginal distributions with correlation
-\rho
and set
(X_{1},X_{2})=(\Phi(Z_{1}),\Phi(Z_{2}))
, where
\Phi
is the standard normal distribution function. Then let
Y|X\sim N(X_{1}-X_{2},0.3^{2})
.

(ii)

Let
(X_{1},X_{2})
have
\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
marginal distributions and dependence structure given by the Clayton copula with parameter 3. For
\mu\in\{0,2,4,\ldots,20\}
let
Y|X
have a Bernoulli distribution with success probability
\{1+e^{-\mu(X_{1}+X_{2}-1)}\}^{-1}
.

(iii)

Generate
(X_{1},X_{2})
as in Setting (i). Then let
Y|X\sim N(-5X_{1}X_{2},0.3^{2})
.

The complete data sample sizes are given by
n=200,600
and the ratio of the incomplete samples sizes to
n
is fixed at
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=10
. Each point in the plots is the result of averaging over
10000
repetitions of the experiment.

The results of these simulations are given in Figure
1
. The plots show the rescaled mean squared error against
\rho
or
\mu
, as appropriate, with error bars showing three standard deviations. There is almost uniform improvement over the complete-case estimator, with the only exceptions being when
\mu=0,2
in setting (ii) where the covariates
(X_{1},X_{2})
are independent of or very weakly dependent on
Y
. In all cases we see that the direct estimators outperform the cross-fitting estimators, though this is less pronounced when
n=600
as compared to
n=200
. Increasing
M
tends to decrease the error of the estimators, as predicted by the asymptotic theory, though there are cases where the cross-fit estimators with a smaller value of
M
perform better as samples are split into fewer pieces. Consequently, the cross-fit
M=3,4
estimators are only shown in the
n=600
results. While the plug-in estimator with MICE performs well in Setting (i), it is often outperformed in the other settings, with error beyond the range of the axes in some cases, mostly due to non-negligible bias; see Appendix
B
for further simulations on this point.

Figure 1:
Estimated rescaled mean squared errors. Settings (i)–(iii) are presented from left to right, while the top row shows results for
n=200
and the bottom row for
n=600
. The solid black line shows results for the complete-case estimator and the dotted black line for MICE. Coloured lines show results for our proposed estimator, with
M=1,2,3,4
corresponding to red, green, blue and cyan, respectively, solid lines corresponding to the direct approach without sample splitting and dotted lines corresponding to the cross-fit estimators. Error bars show three standard deviations.

5
Sampling-bias extensions

5.1
Missing At Random

It is known that when missingness structures are non-monotonic the development of models for mechanisms is challenging and that standard Missing At Random (MAR) assumptions are difficult to work with
(
Robins and Gill, 1997
)
. In this section we work under a type of MAR assumption, sometimes referred to as Covariate Dependent Missingness
(
Li, 2013
)
, where, conditionally on a set of fully-observed variables, the missingness is independent of the remaining variables.

We suppose that
X\sim f
, that
\Omega
encodes the missingness with
\mathbb{S}=\{S\subseteq[d]:p_{S}>0\}
being the collection of possible patterns and that we observe
N
independent and identically distributed copies of
X\circ\Omega
. Here we assume that there exists
S_{0}\subseteq[d]
such that
S_{0}\subseteq S
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
and that we may write
\mathbb{P}(\Omega=\mathbbm{1}_{S}|X=x)=M_{S}(x_{S_{0}})
, so that the missingness mechanism
M_{\mathbb{S}}=(M_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
depends only on those variables in
S_{0}
, which are always observed. We will see that our previous framework naturally extends to this MAR setting. Importantly, we do not need
M_{\mathbb{S}}
to be known, only that it can be estimated sufficiently well.

In order to state our results we write
H_{S}^{S_{0}}
for the subspace of
H_{S}
consisting of those elements
\alpha_{S}
such that
\mathbb{E}\{\alpha_{S}(X_{S})|X_{S_{0}}\}=0
almost surely, we write
H_{\mathbb{S}}^{S_{0}}=\prod_{S\in\mathbb{S}}H_{S}^{S_{0}}
and we define the new objective function

\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}},M_{\mathbb{S}})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{M_{[d]}(X_{S_{0}})}\biggl\{a(X)-a_{S_{0}}(X_{S_{0}})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\biggr\}^{2}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{\alpha_{S}(X_{S})^{2}}{M_{S}(X_{S_{0}})}\biggr].

Minimising this function over
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}^{S_{0}}
can be thought of as fitting generalised ANOVA decompositions separately for each value of
X_{S_{0}}
. The following result establishes a local asymptotic minimax lower bound in this MAR setting. This is proved very similarly to Theorem
8
, with the main differences being the change in the direction of the worst-case perturbations and the fact that we now have random sample sizes within each missingness pattern. Although the missingness mechanism
M_{\mathbb{S}}
may be unknown, it need not be perturbed to achieve a tight lower bound, and it suffices to consider perturbations of
f
as in Section
3.2
.

Theorem 17
.

Suppose that we observe
N
i.i.d. copies of
X\circ\Omega
, as described above, and that
\inf_{x_{S_{0}}}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}M_{S}(x_{S_{0}})>0
. For any measurable estimator sequence
(\theta_{N})
we have

\sup_{I\subset L^{2}}\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\max_{h\in I}\mathbb{E}_{f_{N^{-1/2}h}}\bigl[N\bigl\{\theta_{N}-\theta(f_{N^{-1/2}h})\}^{2}\bigr]\geq\mathrm{Var}\bigl(a_{S_{0}}(X_{S_{0}})\bigr)+\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}^{S_{0}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}},M_{\mathbb{S}}),

where the supremum is taken over all finite subsets
I
of square-integrable functions
h
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

In the remainder of this section we outline the construction of efficient estimators and prove a result under high-level assumptions similar to those that appear in the literature on double robustness. The form of our lower bound and the proof of Theorem
17
suggest that an appropriate oracle estimator is given by

\theta^{*}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl[a_{S_{0}}(X_{i})+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\Omega_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{[d]}\}}}{M_{[d]}(X_{i})}\biggl\{a(X_{i})-a_{S_{0}}(X_{i})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}^{*}(X_{i})\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\Omega_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{S}\}}}{M_{S}(X_{i})}\alpha_{S}^{*}(X_{i})\biggr],

which is unbiased and whose variance is equal to the lower bound. Suppose that we have access to independent data
\mathcal{D}
with which we construct estimators
\widehat{a}_{S_{0}},\widehat{M}_{\mathbb{S}},\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S}}
of the nuisance functions in
\theta^{*}
. Using standard cross-fitting approaches we see that we do not need to make this assumption, as these quantities can be estimated on held-out halves of the data without losing efficiency, but for simplicity we do not make this explicit here. Both
a_{S_{0}}
and
M_{\mathbb{S}}
can be estimated using standard techniques, e.g. kernel nonparametric regression. One way to estimate
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}
would be to partition the sample space of
X_{S_{0}}
into small bins and use the approach of Section
4
within each bin, though we omit the full and explicit construction of such an estimator. Here we simply assume that, if
X
is independent of
\mathcal{D}
and we write

\displaystyle a_{N}

\displaystyle=\max\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{a}_{S_{0}}(X_{S_{0}})-a_{S_{0}}(X_{S_{0}})\bigr\}^{2}\bigr],\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{S})-\alpha_{S}^{*}(X_{S})\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\biggr\}

\displaystyle b_{N}

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{\frac{M_{S}(X_{S_{0}})}{\widehat{M}_{S}(X_{S_{0}})}-1\biggr\}^{2}\biggr],

then we have
\max(a_{N},b_{N},Na_{N}b_{N})\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Given such estimators we consider

\widehat{\theta}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl[\widehat{a}_{S_{0}}(X_{i})+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\Omega_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{[d]}\}}}{\widehat{M}_{[d]}(X_{i})}\biggl\{a(X_{i})-\widehat{a}_{S_{0}}(X_{i})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{i})\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{\Omega_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{S}\}}}{\widehat{M}_{S}(X_{i})}\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{i})\biggr].

Proposition 18
.

Under the conditions of the previous paragraph and the assumptions that
\|a\|_{\infty}<\infty
and
\inf_{x_{S_{0}}}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{+}}M_{S}(x_{S_{0}})>0
, we have that

\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}=O_{p}\biggl(\frac{a_{N}^{1/2}+b_{N}^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}+(a_{N}b_{N})^{1/2}\biggr)=o_{p}(N^{-1/2})

as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

This result implies that such an estimator
\widehat{\theta}
has an asymptotic normal distribution whose variance is equal to the right-hand side of the lower bound in Theorem
17
, and is proved by controlling
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}|
. By slightly strengthening the conditions we could similarly control the second moment of the difference in estimators and thus prove that the mean squared risk of
\widehat{\theta}
is asymptotically equivalent to the mean squared risk of
\theta^{*}
.

5.2
Unknown shift functions

Here we show how the distributional assumptions of our basic model can be relaxed in a different direction to that in Section
5.1
. As previously, we assume that we have access to complete data
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}f
from the target distribution and that we have incomplete data generated according to (
1
). However, rather than assuming that
r_{\mathbb{S}}
is known, we assume that

r_{S}(x_{S})=\exp\bigl(w_{S}^{T}\Psi_{S}(x_{S})\bigr)

for some unknown parameter vector
w_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{S}}
, some
k_{S}\in\mathbb{N}
and some known functions
\Psi_{S}(x_{S})=(\psi_{S,1}(x_{S}),\ldots,\psi_{S,k_{S}}(x_{S}))
. This is a well-studied parametric model for distributional shifts
(
Efron and Tibshirani, 1996
;
Qin, 1998
;
Qiu, Tchetgen Tchetgen and Dobriban, 2024
, e.g.)
. For simplicity, we will assume that the functions
\psi_{S,k}
are all bounded, so that
\|\bar{r}_{S}\|_{\infty}<\infty
as previously assumed. So that the model is identifiable, we assume that there are no
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
w_{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{S}}
such that
w_{S}^{T}\Psi_{S}(\cdot)
is a constant function.

In order to state a lower bound we define the subspace
H_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}=\prod_{S\in\mathbb{S}}H_{S}^{\perp}
of
H_{\mathbb{S}}
given by

H_{S}^{\perp}=\bigl\{\alpha_{S}\in H_{S}:\mathbb{E}\{\alpha_{S}(X_{S})\Psi_{S}(X_{S})\}=0\bigr\}.

Our next result shows that minimising our basic objective function
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
, defined in (
7
), over
H_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
instead of
H_{\mathbb{S}}
gives a lower bound in this setting. While in previous lower bounds it was sufficient to perturb
f
, here we also perturb the unknown parameter vectors
w_{\mathbb{S}}=(w_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
, so we write
P_{n,t,v_{\mathbb{S}}}
for the distribution of our data when the target distribution is
f_{n^{-1/2}t\alpha_{\perp}}
and our distributional shifts are given by
\exp((w_{S}+n^{-1/2}v_{S})^{T}\Psi_{S}(x_{S}))
for
S\in\mathbb{S}
. We see here that, unlike the missingness mechanisms of the previous section, the parameters
w_{\mathbb{S}}
generally cannot be estimated without inflating the variance in estimating
\theta
.

Theorem 19
.

There exists a unique minimiser
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
over
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
. Morever, writing
\alpha^{\perp}=a-\theta-\sum_{S}\alpha_{S}^{\perp}
and
k_{\mathbb{S}}=\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}k_{S}
, for any estimator sequence
(\theta_{n})
we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\mathbb{R}\\
J\subset\mathbb{R}^{k_{\mathbb{S}}}\end{subarray}}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in I\\
v_{\mathbb{S}}\in J\end{subarray}}\,\,\mathbb{E}_{P_{n,t,v_{\mathbb{S}}}}\bigl[n\bigl\{\theta_{n}-\theta(f_{n^{-1/2}t\alpha^{\perp}})\}^{2}\bigr]\geq\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}),

where the supremum is taken over all finite subsets
I
of
\mathbb{R}
and finite subsets
J
of
\mathbb{R}^{k_{\mathbb{S}}}
.

In the remainder of this section we outline the construction of efficient estimators as in the previous section. Here, in light of the proof of Theorem
19
we aim to mimic the oracle estimator

\theta^{*}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{a(X_{i})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\alpha_{S}^{\perp}(X_{i})\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{1}{n_{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\frac{\alpha_{S}^{\perp}(X_{S,j})}{\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S,j})},

where we write
\bar{r}_{S}(x_{S})=\exp(w_{S}^{0}+w_{S}^{T}\Psi_{S}(x_{S}))
and
w_{S}^{0}
normalises the shift function. Suppose that we have access to independent data
\mathcal{D}
with which we construct estimators
(\widehat{w}_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},\widehat{w}_{\mathbb{S}}),\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
of the nuisance parameters in
\theta^{*}
. Again, with cross-fitting one can achieve the same results without held-out data. The
(w_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},w_{\mathbb{S}})
can be estimated using standard density ratio estimation techniques
(
Sugiyama, Suzuki and Kanamori, 2012
, see, e.g.)
. The
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
can be estimated consistently using the gradient descent approach of Section
4
, modified to include projections onto
H_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
rather than just the spaces of mean-zero functions. We introduce the high-level assumptions that, if
X
is independent of
\mathcal{D}
and we write
\widehat{r}_{S}(x_{S})=\exp(\widehat{w}_{S}^{0}+\widehat{w}_{S}^{T}\Psi_{S}(x_{S}))
,

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{1\wedge\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S})}\bigl\{\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{S})-\alpha_{S}^{\perp}(X_{S})\bigr\}^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\max\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1\vee\alpha_{S}^{\perp}(X_{S})^{2}}{1\wedge\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S})}\biggl\{\frac{\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S})}{\widehat{r}_{S}(X_{S})}-1\biggr\}^{2},

\displaystyle\hskip 85.0pt\biggl|\mathbb{E}\biggl[\alpha_{S}^{\perp}(X_{S})\biggl\{\frac{\bar{r}_{S}(X_{S})}{\widehat{r}_{S}(X_{S})}-1-w_{S}^{0}+\widehat{w}_{S}^{0}-(w_{S}-\widehat{w}_{S})^{T}\Psi_{S}(X_{S})\biggr\}\biggr]\biggr|\biggr\},

then we have
\max(a_{n},b_{n},na_{n}b_{n},nb_{n}^{2})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Under appropriate regularity conditions, if the estimators of
(w_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},w_{\mathbb{S}})
are
\sqrt{n}
-consistent then it suffices to have consistent estimators of
\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}
. We can now introduce the final estimator

\widehat{\theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\{a(X_{i})-\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{i})\biggr\}+\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{1}{n_{S}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{S}}\frac{\widehat{\alpha}_{S}(X_{S,j})}{\widehat{r}_{S}(X_{S,j})}.

Proposition 20
.

Under the conditions of the previous paragraph we have that

\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{*}=O_{p}\biggl(\frac{a_{n}^{1/2}+b_{n}^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}+(a_{n}b_{n})^{1/2}+b_{n}\biggr)=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Appendix A
Estimation without sample splitting

In this section we study a more direct estimator of
\theta(f)
that does not rely on splitting our samples, designed for the MCAR case that
r_{S}\equiv 1
for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
. The analysis of such an estimator is technically more challenging and, as a result, we assume that our complete data takes values in
[0,1]^{d}
and make further assumptions on
f
. While our work here concerns a more restricted setting, the explicit form of the estimator may be of independent interest.

We will assume that there exist
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},L_{1},L_{2},c_{0},C_{0}>0
such that the following conditions hold.

(B1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1}
)

For all
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d}
we have

|a(x)-a(x^{\prime})|\leq L_{1}\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{\beta_{1}}.

(13)

(B2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}
)

For all
x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d}
we have

|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|\leq L_{2}\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{\beta_{2}}.

(14)

(B3)(
c_{0},C_{0}
)

For all
x\in[0,1]^{d}
we have

c_{0}\leq f(x)\leq C_{0}.

Assumption (B1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1})
is almost identical to the earlier (A1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1})
, with the only difference being that it only requires smoothness on
[0,1]^{d}
rather than all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It follows from a simple calculation, similar to that in (
90
), that (B2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}
) and (B3)(
c_{0},C_{0}
) together imply (A2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}/c_{0})
. We make these stronger assumptions to facilitate the analysis.

We now turn to the construction of our estimator. Let
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be a kernel function satisfying
\int K=1
,
K(u)=0
when
\|u\|_{\infty}>1/2
, and
K(\xi_{1}u_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}u_{d})=K(u_{1},\ldots,u_{d})
for any
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}\in\{-1,1\}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given
S\in\mathbb{S}
and a bandwidth
h\in(0,1)
write
K^{S}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S^{c}}}K(u_{S},u_{S^{c}}^{\prime})\,du_{S^{c}}^{\prime}
and
K_{h}^{S}(u)=h^{-|S|}K^{S}(u/h)
, as in the previous section. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
h\in(0,1)
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
we define the marginal density estimator

\widehat{f}_{S,h}(x)\equiv\widehat{f}_{S}(x)=\frac{1}{n+n_{S}}\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}K_{h}^{S}(X_{S,i}-x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{h}^{S}(X_{i}-x)\biggr\}.

Given
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}
we write
v_{\mathbf{S}}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+n/n_{S_{j}})^{-1}
and define the random function
\widehat{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m+1}
by

\widehat{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m+1})=a(x_{1})\biggl\{\frac{K_{h}^{S_{1}}(x_{2}-x_{1})}{\widehat{f}_{S_{1}}(x_{1})}-1\biggr\}\ldots\biggl\{\frac{K_{h}^{S_{m}}(x_{m+1}-x_{m})}{\widehat{f}_{S_{m}}(x_{m})}-1\biggr\}.

Given a choice of truncation level
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and bandwidth
h\in(0,1)
we take step size
\eta=|\mathbb{S}|^{-1}
as in Section
4
and define our estimator to be

\displaystyle\check{\theta}_{h}^{M}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}(-1)^{m}\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}}v_{(S_{1},\ldots,S_{m-1})}b_{M,\eta}(m)\frac{1}{(n)_{m+1}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{m+1}}\widehat{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{m+1}}),

where we interpret the
m=0
term as
\widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{CC}}
. The following is our main result of this section.

Theorem 21
.

Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in(0,1]
and
L_{1},L_{2},c_{0},C_{0}>0
and suppose that
a
and
f
satisfy (B1)(
\beta_{1},L_{1}
), (B2)(
\beta_{2},L_{2}
) and (B3)(
c_{0},C_{0}
). Suppose further that
\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\lambda_{S}<\infty
and that
\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\bar{r}_{S}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. Then there exists a constant
C\equiv C(d,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},L_{1},L_{2},c_{0},C_{0},\|K\|_{\infty})
such that

\mathbb{E}_{f}[n\{\check{\theta}_{h}^{M}-\theta(f)\}^{2}]\rightarrow\inf_{\alpha_{\mathbb{S}}\in H_{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{\mathbb{S}})

whenever
h
and
M
are chosen to satisfy
M\rightarrow\infty
,
hC^{M}\rightarrow 0
and
(C/h^{d})^{2M+2}/n\rightarrow 0
.

This result is a consequence of the finite-sample results Propositions
22
and
23
below and Proposition
12
and the second part of Proposition
13
above. The requirement on the bandwidth
h
is stronger than for our sample-splitting estimator in Section
4
, but it is sufficient to prove that our direct estimator achieves the minimal asymptotic variance.

Theorem
21
is proved by showing that our estimator can be approximated successively by two oracle estimators. The second of these is
\theta^{*,(M)}
, defined previously in (
12
), but the first is new. In order to introduce this new oracle estimator we must introduce some population-level quantities. We will write
f_{S,h}(x)\equiv f_{S,h}(x_{S})=\mathbb{E}\{\widehat{f}_{S,h}(x)\}=(K_{h}^{S}\ast f)(x)
for a smoothed version of the marginal density
f_{S}
. Given
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}
we define an oracle version of
\widehat{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}
by

k_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m+1})=a(x_{1})\biggl\{\frac{K_{h}^{S_{1}}(x_{2}-x_{1})}{f_{S_{1},h}(x_{1})}-1\biggr\}\ldots\biggl\{\frac{K_{h}^{S_{m}}(x_{m+1}-x_{m})}{f_{S_{m},h}(x_{m})}-1\biggr\}

and further write
\bar{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(x)=\mathbb{E}\{k_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},x)\}
. We may now define the oracle statistic

\theta_{h}^{*,M}=\widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{CC}}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}(-1)^{m}\sum_{\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}}v_{\mathbf{S}}b_{M,\eta}(m)\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(X_{i})-\frac{1}{n_{S_{m}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S_{m}}}\bar{k}_{h}^{\mathbf{S}}(X_{S_{m},i})\biggr\}.

Our next result shows that this indeed provides an approximation to
\check{\theta}_{h}^{M}
.

Proposition 22
.

Suppose that there exists
c_{0}>0
